--- PROTOCOL.md ---
# Upstream filing protocol

**Ruled by the project owner 2026-09-01, after auditing the 2026-08-26 round.**

This binds every filing to an XRPLF repository. `make check-filing` enforces the
mechanical parts; the rest is judgement and is listed so it can be checked by a
person.

---

## Why this exists

Seven items were filed on 2026-08-26. An adversarial review run **after
submission** found **13 defects**, six graded *would embarrass*. Four of five
first-round issues needed same-day corrections in public.

The 2026-09-01 audit re-verified all six open filings at upstream HEAD: **every
claim still stands and none is stale.** So the content was recoverable. What was
not recoverable is the impression left by correcting four public issues within
hours of opening them.

⚠ **The defect was the order of operations, not the research.** Everything below
follows from that.

---

## The two commands

- **`/upstream-issue`** — drafts a filing from a verified finding.
- **`/issue-review`** — attacks the draft before it is sent. **Rule 1 gates on it.**

⚠ Both are slash commands in `.claude/commands/`, matching this project's three
existing ones — **user-invoked, never model-invoked**, because filing needs
authorisation every time.

⚠ **Both read the target repository's issue template first**, and that outranks
anything here. **Measured 2026-09-01:** `XRPLF/rippled` sets
`blank_issues_enabled: false` and offers only `bug_report.md` and
`feature_request.md`; `XRPL-Standards`, `xrpl-dev-portal` and `mpt-crypto` have
none. ⚠⚠ **`rippled#8118` fit neither rippled template, was filed anyway, and
was closed without comment.** Reading the template first would have stopped that
filing before it was written — which makes it the cheapest rule here and the one
that was missing.

---

## The five rules

### 1. Adversarial review runs BEFORE filing, and it gates

No filing is sent until an independent adversarial pass has run against the
**draft text and the frozen primary sources**, and every finding is resolved.

⚠ **A review that runs after submission cannot prevent anything.** It can only
produce a correction, and a correction is a public record that the filing was
not ready.

**The reviewer is given the draft and the sources, not the conclusion.**

### 2. A filing is at most 300 words, with one concrete ask

Counted over the issue body, excluding code blocks, quoted source and the
disclosure footer.

⚠ **`WORD_LIMIT` in `tools/check-filing.ts` is what enforces this, and the gate
prints the number it used.** This heading states the rule; that constant is the
instrument. If the two ever disagree, the gate is right and this line is the
defect — and neither the skills nor a draft may restate the number at all.

- **One ask per issue.** If there are five asks, that is five issues or one
  errata list with five clearly numbered items and no argument between them.
- **Evidence is linked, not inlined.** A permalink to a line range beats a
  quotation; a quotation beats a paragraph explaining the quotation.
- **State the defect, the evidence, and the fix.** Nothing else.

⚠ The 2026-08-26 filings averaged **~750 words**. The dev-portal one was a
two-sentence documentation fix in six sections. A maintainer who cannot find the
ask in fifteen seconds will not look for it.

### 3. No "positives" filings to a bug tracker

An issue tracker is for things that need doing. Appreciation is not.

⚠ **And on `rippled` it is not even accepted**: `blank_issues_enabled: false`,
with only a bug report and a feature request offered.

⚠ `rippled#8118` was *"four design decisions that were better than they
looked"*. It needed a same-day correction because its central claim was false —
the spec advertises the property **twice**, in wording close to what the filing
presented as its own reading — and a core contributor **closed it without
comment**.

**Positive observations still get captured** in `UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md`, and they
still hold the higher bar in `CLAUDE.md`. They reach a team through a channel
meant for them, or not at all.

### 4. No negative claim without its recorded search

*"There is no field"*, *"nothing documents this"*, *"not exported"*, *"nobody
has called this out"* — each is a claim about a whole corpus, and one search
settles it.

The search command and its result go **in the draft**, next to the claim.

⚠ Two rows died this way. **U-15** quoted a sentence XLS-0096 has never
contained. **`rippled#8118` item 1** said a property was not advertised anywhere,
and the spec states it twice. `CLAIMS.md` **P-12** and
`tools/check-negative-claims.ts` came out of the first; this rule extends the
gate to drafts.

### 5. Plain English, short sentences

The reader is a maintainer skimming a queue, and English may not be their first
language.

- Short sentences. One idea each.
- Say **`fail_hard`**, not `tapFailHard`. ⚠ Project shorthand is not an API name
  and it has escaped into a public filing once already (`CLAIMS.md` E-156).
- No rhetorical build-up, no *"interestingly"*, no *"it is worth noting"*.
- Define an abbreviation on first use or drop it.
- ⚠ **Do not carry this repository's emphasis style outward.** The warning signs
  and bold blocks are internal instruments; in a filing they read as shouting.

---

## Before sending — the checklist

1. `make check-filing FILE=<draft>` passes.
2. An adversarial review has run on the draft and every finding is resolved.
3. Every citation re-fetched at source **today**, with the ref recorded.
4. Every negative claim carries its search.
5. The Trail of Bits April 2026 review has been checked — ⚠ several things this
   project first recorded as findings were already found and fixed there.
6. The ask is one sentence and appears in the first paragraph.
7. Authorisation from the project owner **for this filing**. ⚠ Authorisation is
   never standing; it is per filing, per session.

---

## After sending

- Archive the exact submitted text under `docs/upstream-filings/<date>/`. A
  GitHub issue can be edited; the archive is what the register points at.
- Record the issue URL in `UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md` and move the row to `Filed`.
- ⚠ **If a correction becomes necessary, post it as a comment and leave the
  original body alone.** Editing the body makes the thread incoherent — the
  correction then refers to text that no longer exists — and GitHub shows the
  edit history publicly anyway.

---

## What this protocol does not fix

- ⚠ **It does not make a filing welcome.** Six of seven from 2026-08-26 have had
  no response after six days. That is normal for these repositories and is not
  evidence of anything, in either direction.
- ⚠ **`check-filing` is lexical.** It counts words, finds unsearched negatives by
  their common spellings, and flags long sentences. **It cannot tell whether a
  claim is true**, and a claim phrased in a way it does not recognise is
  invisible to it. The review in rule 1 is the instrument for that; this gate
  only stops the shapes that have already gone wrong twice.
--- REVIEW-INSTRUCTIONS.md ---
# Upstream issue review

Review the draft as a skeptical maintainer **and** as a fact-checker. Produce an
edited version.

⚠ **Read-only against GitHub. Do not create, submit, edit or close anything.**

## ⚠⚠ STOP — did you write this draft?

**If this draft was produced in this session, or by you, REFUSE.**

Say plainly: *"I drafted this. Rule 1's review has not run and I cannot run
it."* Then stop. **Do not review it, do not rewrite it, do not report a word
count.** Producing an edited version here is worse than doing nothing, because
it makes rule 1 look satisfied when no independent pass has happened.

⚠ **The writer hands off for this reason; both halves of the handshake must
refuse, or the gate is closed by whichever half is invoked second.** The first
version of these two commands fixed only the writer, and an independent review
found that running them back to back in one session still produced a
"completed" review.

⚠ **This project has measured what the difference is worth.** A self-review of
these two commands found four defects and called them fixed. An independent pass
then found **six more** — one of them introduced by the self-review, and one it
had "fixed" in a single place while a second copy stood. **A further independent
pass found four more, two of them in those fixes.**

**A fresh session, a different model, or a person. Not you.**

---

## 0. First, the two cheap rejections — before any source is opened

⚠ **These cost one API call each and can end the review immediately.** Verifying
claims against primary sources and the Trail of Bits appendix is the expensive
part; do not spend it on a draft that cannot be filed anyway.

**Right venue?** Confirm the destination repository exists and is owned by the
org the draft assumes. ⚠ **Testing this on U-54 found exactly that defect** — the
wallet standard is not in `XRPLF/XRPL-Standards` at all. **A correct finding sent
to the wrong repository is still a wasted filing**, and no evidence check catches
it.

**Right shape?** Read the target's `.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/` **and decode its
`config.yml`** — listing filenames does not establish a policy.

```sh
gh api repos/<owner>/<repo>/contents/.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/config.yml \
  --jq '.content' | base64 -d
```

⚠ **`blank_issues_enabled: false` means only the listed shapes are accepted.**
`rippled` sets it, `rippled#8118` fit neither template, and it was closed without
comment. **If the draft fits no accepted template, stop here and say so.**

---

## ⚠ Then correctness, and this is the whole point

A concision pass would not have caught **a single one** of the thirteen defects
found in the 2026-08-26 round. Every one was a claim that was **false or
overstated**, and counting words finds none of those.

**So attack the claims before you touch the prose.**

### Verify every claim against the primary source, now

- Open the source the draft cites. **Do not trust the draft's quotation** — a
  filed row once quoted a sentence the specification has never contained
  (`UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md` U-15).
- Check line numbers and section numbers still resolve. Sources move.
- ⚠ **Every negative claim needs its search, and you run it.** *"There is no
  such field"*, *"not called out anywhere"*, *"not exported"* — one search
  settles it. **`rippled#8118` said a property was not advertised anywhere; the
  specification states it twice**, once in wording close to what the draft
  presented as its own reading.
- Check whether upstream already fixed it. **Four rows died as stale gaps** —
  `UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md` U-3, U-8, U-9, U-10 — each describing something already closed.
- Check the Trail of Bits April 2026 review. Several things this project first
  recorded as findings were already found and fixed there. Read Appendices B to D
  too: the fix review does not cover them, and F-19 was in Appendix B
  (`CLAIMS.md` E-296).

### Separate the three layers

**Demonstrated** — you ran it or read it at source.
**Inferred** — it follows, but nobody checked.
**Proposed** — what the draft wants done.

⚠ A draft that presents the second or third as the first is the defect that
needed four public corrections. Say which layer each claim is on.

⚠ **Do not let the draft's framing set yours.** Ask what would have to be true
for the claim to be *wrong*, and go and check that.

## Then the maintainer's read

**Actionable?** Can a maintainer tell what to investigate, decide or change?

**Scoped?** One independently actionable problem, or several disguised as one?

**Skimmable?** Headings, bullets, code blocks, tables. Conclusion before
evidence.

**Something to do?** ⚠ A positives-only filing does not belong on a bug tracker
at all — reject it rather than editing it.

## Delete

Essay introductions, throat-clearing, praise before the report, rhetorical
questions, speculative motivation about maintainer intent, repeated conclusions,
research narration, background a maintainer already has, and any restatement of
the same point in summary and conclusion.

**Also delete, specifically for this project:** project shorthand
(`tapFailHard`), internal register references (`CLAIMS.md`, `E-nnn`,
`REVIEW-nnn`), and this repository's ⚠-and-bold emphasis style.

## Rewrite

Produce the edited version. **Aim to cut at least 20% without losing
load-bearing evidence.** If the draft is already tight, say so and do not cut
mechanically.

Run `make check-filing FILE=<draft>` on the result.

## Report

1. **Claims that did not survive verification** — with what you ran.
2. **Claims that need a search that has not been run.**
3. **Layer corrections** — demonstrated / inferred / proposed.
4. **The edited version.**
5. **Word count before and after.**

⚠ **If any claim failed verification, say plainly that the draft is not ready to
file.** Rule 1 gates on this. It is better to lose a filing than to correct one
in public.
--- draft.md ---
TITLE: [Build] build-wasm.sh links C++ with emcc, which fails from emsdk 6.0.6 on
REPO: XRPLF/mpt-crypto

---

`build-wasm.sh` compiles `src/utility/mpt_utility.cpp` as C++, then links the module with plain `emcc`. From emsdk 6.0.6, `emcc` no longer links the C++ runtime by default, so the link fails with the C++ runtime missing. CI did not catch it because it pins emsdk 6.0.2, and the WASM job [passed at `3baaaac`](https://github.com/XRPLF/mpt-crypto/actions/runs/32380884181). The script's own prerequisites line names no version, so a build with any emsdk I tried from 6.0.6 on fails.

**Suggested fix:** link with `em++` on the three link lines, as Emscripten's changelog recommends.

**Cause**

Emscripten's [ChangeLog for 6.0.6](https://github.com/emscripten-core/emscripten/blob/6.0.6/ChangeLog.md#L23-L25):

> `DEFAULT_TO_CXX` is now disabled by default. This means that `em++` is now required when linking C++ programs, matching the behavior of clang and gcc. The old behavior is still available using `-sDEFAULT_TO_CXX`. (#11121)

At `3baaaac`:

- The C++ object is compiled with `em++` ([line 272](https://github.com/XRPLF/mpt-crypto/blob/3baaaac33dadec3e649846e487f452c5b6b2d825/.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh#L272)).
- It is linked with `emcc` ([lines 363, 365 and 374](https://github.com/XRPLF/mpt-crypto/blob/3baaaac33dadec3e649846e487f452c5b6b2d825/.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh#L363-L374)).
- CI pins [emsdk 6.0.2](https://github.com/XRPLF/mpt-crypto/blob/3baaaac33dadec3e649846e487f452c5b6b2d825/.github/workflows/build-wasm.yml#L38-L43).
- The script asks only for [*"Emscripten SDK … on PATH"*](https://github.com/XRPLF/mpt-crypto/blob/3baaaac33dadec3e649846e487f452c5b6b2d825/.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh#L19).

**What I ran**

`./.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh` at `3baaaac`, then `test-wasm.sh` where the table says so. Each build used a fresh source tree and its own emsdk. macOS arm64, 2026-10-18. I did not run Linux.

```text
emsdk                as shipped          em++ on the 3 link lines     -sDEFAULT_TO_CXX on the 3 link lines
6.0.2                builds, tests pass  builds, tests pass,          builds, tests pass,
                                         output byte-identical        output byte-identical
6.0.3, 6.0.4, 6.0.5  builds, tests pass  not run                      not run
6.0.6                fails at line 363   builds, tests pass           builds, tests pass
6.0.10               fails at line 363   builds, tests pass           builds, tests pass
6.0.12 (newest)      fails at line 363   builds, tests pass           builds, tests pass
```

The 6.0.6 and 6.0.10 failure. At 6.0.12 it also lists `__cxa_guard_acquire` and `__cxa_guard_release`.

```text
wasm-ld: error: …/mpt_crypto.wasm.lto.o: undefined symbol: operator new(unsigned long)
  (and 7 more: operator delete, __cxa_allocate_exception, __cxa_throw,
   typeinfo / vtable / ~length_error for std::length_error, std::logic_error::logic_error)
emcc: warning: link failed with undefined C++ symbols. Try linking with 'em++' or passing '-sDEFAULT_TO_CXX'
```

All three lines need the change. With only lines 363 and 365 changed, 6.0.6 still fails at line 374.

c60a60cc82eac5f7446480e0363105b8ace44631
--- pinned build-wasm.sh ---
     1	#!/usr/bin/env bash
     2	# build-wasm.sh — Build the shipped mpt-crypto WebAssembly module.
     3	#
     4	# Produces the JS-consumable artifacts that downstream JS/TS libraries
     5	# (e.g. xrpl.js) vendor:
     6	#   emcc_out/mpt_crypto.js      (MODULARIZE CommonJS glue — Node require)
     7	#   emcc_out/mpt_crypto.mjs     (EXPORT_ES6 ESM glue, web+node — Node import)
     8	#   emcc_out/mpt_crypto.web.mjs (EXPORT_ES6 ESM glue, web only — bundlers/browsers)
     9	#   emcc_out/mpt_crypto.wasm    (the compiled module, curated exports)
    10	#
    11	# Builds ONLY the module (not tests) — the companion test-wasm.sh validates it
    12	# by running the crypto suite under Node; the CI workflow runs both in order.
    13	# It has a dedicated emcc link (rather than CMake) because the wasm module needs
    14	# MODULARIZE/exports/WASM_BIGINT.
    15	#
    16	# As a side effect it builds the wasm-target secp256k1 + OpenSSL static libs
    17	# into emcc_build/, which test-wasm.sh reuses.
    18	#
    19	# Prerequisites: Emscripten SDK (emcc, em++, emcmake, emmake) on PATH.
    20	#
    21	# Usage (paths resolve to the repo root, so run from anywhere):
    22	#   ./.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh          # full build (deps + module)
    23	#   ./.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh --clean  # remove build artifacts and rebuild
    24	
    25	set -euo pipefail
    26	
    27	ROOT_DIR="$(cd "$(dirname "$0")/../.." && pwd)"
    28	BUILD_DIR="${ROOT_DIR}/emcc_build"
    29	OUT_DIR="${ROOT_DIR}/emcc_out"
    30	CONAN_LOCK="${ROOT_DIR}/conan.lock"
   250	
   251	# Collect the objects for THIS source set explicitly and link that list (not a
   252	# ${OBJ_DIR}/*.o glob), so a renamed or deleted source can't leave a stale .o
   253	# behind for the link to silently pick up. Caveat: the -nt check compares only
   254	# the source's mtime, not its headers — after editing a header, rebuild with
   255	# --clean (or delete emcc_build/obj) to force recompilation.
   256	OBJECTS=()
   257	for f in "${ROOT_DIR}"/src/*.c; do
   258	    name="$(basename "$f" .c)"
   259	    obj="${OBJ_DIR}/${name}.o"
   260	    OBJECTS+=("${obj}")
   261	    if [[ "$f" -nt "${obj}" ]]; then
   262	        echo "  CC  ${name}.c"
   263	        emcc ${CFLAGS} -c "$f" -o "${obj}"
   264	    fi
   265	done
   266	
   267	name="mpt_utility"
   268	obj="${OBJ_DIR}/${name}.o"
   269	OBJECTS+=("${obj}")
   270	if [[ "${ROOT_DIR}/src/utility/${name}.cpp" -nt "${obj}" ]]; then
   271	    echo "  CXX ${name}.cpp"
   272	    em++ ${CFLAGS} -std=c++17 -c "${ROOT_DIR}/src/utility/${name}.cpp" -o "${obj}"
   273	fi
   274	
   275	# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   276	# 5. Link final WASM
   277	# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   278	log "Linking mpt_crypto.wasm"
   279	
   280	EXPORTS="_malloc,_free"
   281	EXPORTS="${EXPORTS},_mpt_secp256k1_context"
   282	# _mpt_generate_keypair intentionally NOT exported: ElGamal keys come from
   283	# ripple-keypairs (a separate secp256k1 seed), not the WASM CSPRNG.
   284	EXPORTS="${EXPORTS},_mpt_generate_blinding_factor"
   285	EXPORTS="${EXPORTS},_mpt_encrypt_amount,_mpt_decrypt_amount"
   350	# glues from the identical objects — they all wrap the SAME mpt_crypto.wasm:
   351	#   - mpt_crypto.js      : MODULARIZE CommonJS   (require()'d by the CJS build / Node)
   352	#   - mpt_crypto.mjs     : EXPORT_ES6, web+node  (Node `import`; its Node branch runs
   353	#                          `await import("node:module")` to read the .wasm from disk)
   354	#   - mpt_crypto.web.mjs : EXPORT_ES6, web only  (bundlers/browsers) — the same glue
   355	#                          built with ENVIRONMENT=web,worker instead of web,node, so it
   356	#                          carries NO Node branch and thus no `node:` imports. Browser
   357	#                          bundlers reject the `node:` URI scheme at build time even
   358	#                          though that code is dead in a browser, so the package's
   359	#                          `browser` export must point here, not at mpt_crypto.mjs.
   360	# Both ESM glues use `new URL('mpt_crypto.wasm', import.meta.url)` so bundlers
   361	# auto-emit the .wasm as an asset instead of a runtime read. Keep the three links in
   362	# lockstep — they must wrap the byte-identical module.
   363	emcc "${LINK_FLAGS[@]}" -o "${OUT_DIR}/mpt_crypto.js"
   364	wasm_sha_cjs="$(sha256_of "${OUT_DIR}/mpt_crypto.wasm")"
   365	emcc "${LINK_FLAGS[@]}" -sEXPORT_ES6=1 -o "${OUT_DIR}/mpt_crypto.mjs"
   366	wasm_sha_esm="$(sha256_of "${OUT_DIR}/mpt_crypto.wasm")"
   367	# Browser-only glue. -sENVIRONMENT=web,worker (overriding web,node) drops the Node
   368	# code path and its `node:` imports. Emscripten names the .wasm after the -o basename,
   369	# so link under the shared `mpt_crypto` name in a temp dir — the glue then references
   370	# `mpt_crypto.wasm`, the one we ship — and move just the glue out. Linking straight to
   371	# mpt_crypto.web.mjs would emit and reference a separate mpt_crypto.web.wasm.
   372	web_tmp="$(mktemp -d)"
   373	trap 'rm -rf "${web_tmp}"' EXIT
   374	emcc "${LINK_FLAGS[@]}" -sEXPORT_ES6=1 -sENVIRONMENT=web,worker -o "${web_tmp}/mpt_crypto.mjs"
   375	wasm_sha_web="$(sha256_of "${web_tmp}/mpt_crypto.wasm")"
   376	mv "${web_tmp}/mpt_crypto.mjs" "${OUT_DIR}/mpt_crypto.web.mjs"
   377	
   378	# The browser glue exists precisely so browser bundlers never see a `node:` import; the
   379	# Node smoke test can't catch a regression (Node resolves node:module fine), so assert
   380	# the invariant at build time. Matches emscripten's dynamic `import("node:…")` and any
   381	# static `from "node:…"`, but not the benign `{…,node:…}` FS object literals.
   382	if grep -Eq 'import\(["'"'"']node:|from[[:space:]]*["'"'"']node:' "${OUT_DIR}/mpt_crypto.web.mjs"; then
   383	    echo "ERROR: mpt_crypto.web.mjs leaked a node: import — browser bundlers will reject it" >&2
   384	    exit 1
   385	fi

### .github/scripts/build-wasm.sh

     2	# build-wasm.sh — Build the shipped mpt-crypto WebAssembly module.
     3	#
     4	# Produces the JS-consumable artifacts that downstream JS/TS libraries
     5	# (e.g. xrpl.js) vendor:
     6	#   emcc_out/mpt_crypto.js      (MODULARIZE CommonJS glue — Node require)
     7	#   emcc_out/mpt_crypto.mjs     (EXPORT_ES6 ESM glue, web+node — Node import)
     8	#   emcc_out/mpt_crypto.web.mjs (EXPORT_ES6 ESM glue, web only — bundlers/browsers)
     9	#   emcc_out/mpt_crypto.wasm    (the compiled module, curated exports)
    10	#

    12	# by running the crypto suite under Node; the CI workflow runs both in order.

    14	# MODULARIZE/exports/WASM_BIGINT.

    17	# into emcc_build/, which test-wasm.sh reuses.

    20	#

    22	#   ./.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh          # full build (deps + module)
    23	#   ./.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh --clean  # remove build artifacts and rebuild

   351	#   - mpt_crypto.js      : MODULARIZE CommonJS   (require()'d by the CJS build / Node)
   352	#   - mpt_crypto.mjs     : EXPORT_ES6, web+node  (Node `import`; its Node branch runs

   368	# code path and its `node:` imports. Emscripten names the .wasm after the -o basename,

   378	# The browser glue exists precisely so browser bundlers never see a `node:` import; the
   379	# Node smoke test can't catch a regression (Node resolves node:module fine), so assert
   380	# the invariant at build time. Matches emscripten's dynamic `import("node:…")` and any
   381	# static `from "node:…"`, but not the benign `{…,node:…}` FS object literals.
   382	if grep -Eq 'import\(["'"'"']node:|from[[:space:]]*["'"'"']node:' "${OUT_DIR}/mpt_crypto.web.mjs"; then
   383	    echo "ERROR: mpt_crypto.web.mjs leaked a node: import — browser bundlers will reject it" >&2
   384	    exit 1
   385	fi
### .github/workflows/build-wasm.yml
     1	# ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
     2	# Build WASM Module
     3	#
     4	# Builds the mpt-crypto C library as a single portable WebAssembly module
     5	# (mpt_crypto.js CJS + mpt_crypto.mjs Node-ESM + mpt_crypto.web.mjs browser-ESM + mpt_crypto.wasm)
     6	# for JS/TS consumers (xrpl.js). There is NO OS x arch matrix: one wasm32
     7	# artifact runs in every browser and in Node/Deno/Bun on any
     8	# platform/endianness.
     9	#
    10	# The artifact is validated before upload: .github/scripts/test-wasm.sh compiles
    11	# the C crypto test suite to wasm and runs it under Node (ctest), then smoke-tests
    12	# the shipped glue. The smoke test runs against the STAGED bundle, so a file
    13	# dropped from the bundle fails CI instead of shipping green.
    14	#
    15	# Invoked via `workflow_call` from build.yml — on every PR and push to main (so
    16	# the build+test path is exercised), and on tag push (so build.yml can attach the
    17	# bundle to the release, which JS consumers download).
    18	#
    19	# The `build` job uploads `mpt-crypto-wasm-bundle` (mpt_crypto.js + .mjs + .web.mjs + .wasm),
    20	# which the caller downloads and attaches to the GitHub release.
    21	# ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    22	
    23	name: Build WASM Module
    24	
    25	on:
    26	  workflow_call:
    27	
    28	jobs:
    29	  build:
    30	    name: Build + test WASM module

{"baseRefName":"main","body":"### Why\n`mpt-crypto` is used by XRPL client SDKs/libraries in two ways: native shared libraries (xrpl4j, xrpl-py) and a WebAssembly module (xrpl.js). PR #56 gave the **native** libraries a full CI story — build per platform, run `ctest`, bundle, and attach to the GitHub release. The **WASM** module had no equivalent: no CI build, no tests, and no released artifact.\n\nThis PR brings WASM to parity, so JS/TS consumers can pull a reproducibly-built, tested wasm module from tagged releases — the same guarantee xrpl4j/xrpl-py already get for the native libs.\n\n## What it adds\n```\nbuild-wasm.yml  (one ubuntu job, called by build.yml)\n   │\n   ├─ setup emsdk 6.0.2 + Node 22\n   ├─ build-wasm.sh   → compiles secp256k1 + OpenSSL + mpt-crypto, then links the\n   │                    glue TWICE → emcc_out/mpt_crypto.{js, mjs, wasm}\n   │                      • mpt_crypto.js   MODULARIZE CommonJS glue\n   │                      • mpt_crypto.mjs  EXPORT_ES6 ES-module glue\n   │                      • mpt_crypto.wasm the single shared module both glues wrap\n   ├─ test-wasm.sh    → (1) compiles the crypto test suite to wasm, runs it under\n   │                    Node via ctest; (2) smoke-tests BOTH shipped glues     ← gate\n   └─ bundle + upload → mpt-crypto-wasm-bundle  (js + mjs + wasm)\n                                        │\n   build.yml `release` job (on tag) ────┘\n        needs [build-and-test, native-binaries, wasm]\n        → attaches mpt-crypto-wasm-<tag>.tar.gz to the GitHub release (next to the native bundle)\n```\n\nWASM is a single portable target, so there's no OS×arch matrix — one wasm32 module runs in every browser and in Node/Deno on any platform.\n\n## Dual glue: one wasm, CommonJS + ES module\nThe wasm module is consumed by xrpl.js, which ships as a **dual CJS+ESM package**. To serve both without shipping the wasm twice, `build-wasm.sh` links the glue **twice from the identical object files**, both wrapping the **same** `mpt_crypto.wasm`:\n\n| Output | emcc flags | Consumer |\n|---|---|---|\n| `mpt_crypto.js` | `MODULARIZE` (CommonJS) | Node / Jest (`require`) — reads the `.wasm` off disk |\n| `mpt_crypto.mjs` | `-sEXPORT_ES6=1` (ES module) | bundlers / browsers (`import`) — locates the wasm via `new URL('mpt_crypto.wasm', import.meta.url)`, which webpack/rollup turn into an emitted asset |\n\nThe ESM glue is what lets Confidential MPT run in the browser: the CommonJS glue statically `require`s `node:fs`/`node:crypto` and can't be bundled, whereas the `new URL(import.meta.url)` form makes bundlers emit the wasm as a fetchable asset. Both glues are byte-for-byte the same ABI over the same module — shipping the trio together keeps the glue and wasm in lockstep.\n\n## File by file\n| File | Role |\n|---|---|\n| `.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh` | **Builds the module.** `emcc` link that statically bundles secp256k1 + a stripped OpenSSL, then emits **both** glues (`mpt_crypto.js` MODULARIZE + `mpt_crypto.mjs` EXPORT_ES6) over the same `mpt_crypto.wasm`. Reads dep versions from `conan.lock`; verifies dependency integrity (pinned secp256k1 commit + OpenSSL tarball SHA-256); forces static secp256k1 (`-DBUILD_SHARED_LIBS=OFF`); exports only the symbols consumers use. |\n| `.github/scripts/test-wasm.sh` | **Validates it.** (1) Compiles the C crypto test suite to wasm32 and runs each test under Node via `ctest`, reusing the deps `build-wasm.sh` just built. (2) Smoke-tests **both shipped glues** the way consumers load them (`require` the `.js`, `import` the `.mjs`): instantiate, init the secp256k1 context, then `malloc` + run one export + read `HEAPU8` — so a bad `-s` flag or ABI break fails here, not downstream. |\n| `.github/workflows/build-wasm.yml` | **The CI workflow.** Reusable (`workflow_call`), single ubuntu job, no matrix. Runs the two scripts, bundles the output (js + mjs + wasm), uploads `mpt-crypto-wasm-bundle`. |\n| `.github/workflows/build.yml` | **Wires it in.** Adds a `wasm` job beside `native-binaries`, adds it to `release.needs`, and attaches the wasm tarball on tag push. |\n| `.gitignore` | Ignores the emscripten build outputs (`emcc_build/`, `emcc_out/`). |\n\n## Dependency integrity\nThe native build gets integrity from Conan (`conan.lock` revisions + source SHA-256). This WASM path fetches secp256k1 (git tag) and OpenSSL (release tarball) directly, so it verifies them explicitly to match that guarantee:\n- **secp256k1** — the checked-out commit is verified against a pinned SHA (a git tag is mutable; the commit is not).\n- **OpenSSL** — the tarball's SHA-256 is verified against a pinned hash before extraction.\n\n## How it's tested\n`test-wasm.sh` runs two layers, both gating the `release` job:\n1. **Crypto** — compiles mpt-crypto's C crypto test suite to wasm32 and runs all 11 tests under Node via `ctest`.\n2. **Glue / ABI** — loads each shipped glue the consumer way (`require` the `.js`, `import` the `.mjs`) and exercises the marshalling contract end to end (secp256k1 context init + `malloc` / `mpt_generate_blinding_factor` / `HEAPU8`).\n\nSo a wasm build whose crypto misbehaves — or whose glue/ABI a bad emcc flag has broken — can't --- mpt-crypto#145 ---
{"baseRefName":"main","body":"Summary\n\nAdds _generate_canonical_encrypted_zero to the WASM export list. Part of #141.\n\nThe canonical encrypted zero is a deliberately public property per XLS-0096 section 9.4: everything the derivation needs (holder encryption key, account ID, issuance ID) is public ledger data, so recomputing EncZero and byte-comparing it against a stored ciphertext is how an outside observer determines that a balance is exactly 0. The function exists in the C library but stopped at the WASM boundary, so consumers of the published package had to reimplement the hash-and-rejection-sample themselves. That reimplementation failing to track ours would report \"not canonical zero\" for a balance that is one, which is the failure that looks unremarkable.\n\nThe output is a secp256k1_pubkey pair, so it serializes with the existing _mpt_serialize_ec_pair like _secp256k1_elgamal_add / _secp256k1_elgamal_subtract. The input is a single 33-byte holder key, which _mpt_make_ec_pair (66-byte pairs) can't parse, so this also exports _secp256k1_ec_pubkey_parse. The export entry carries a rationale comment in the style the rest of the list uses.\n\nThe thin TypeScript wrapper the issue also asks for belongs in @xrplf/mpt-crypto rather than here, so this is only the export half.\n\nTesting\n\nI don't have emsdk locally, so I have not run the WASM build; CI covers it. What I did check is that the symbol name is right: nm on a local static build shows `T _generate_canonical_encrypted_zero`, external with the leading underscore emscripten expects, and bash -n on the modified script is clean.\n","comments":[],"commits":[{"authoredDate":"2026-09-17T22:21:27Z","authors":[{"email":"redacted@example.invalid","id":"U_kgDODnE2TA","login":"tesseract-ripple","name":"the user"}],"committedDate":"2026-10-17T07:18:35Z","messageBody":"XLS-0096 section 9.4 makes the canonical encrypted zero a deliberately\npublic property: with the holder encryption key, account ID and issuance\nID, all public ledger data, anyone can recompute EncZero and compare it\nagainst a stored ciphertext to learn that a balance is exactly 0. The\nsymbol stopped at the WASM boundary, so consumers of the published\npackage had to reimplement the derivation to check it, which fails\nsilently if the two ever drift.\n\nPart of #141 (the npm wrapper is still needed).","messageHeadline":"ci: export generate_canonical_encrypted_zero to WASM","oid":"c2c78f8e9193a6f22059e488809be64cb8ae4f0d"},{"authoredDate":"2026-10-17T07:18:35Z","authors":[{"email":"redacted@example.invalid","id":"U_kgDODnE2TA","login":"tesseract-ripple","name":"the user"}],"committedDate":"2026-10-17T07:18:35Z","messageBody":"generate_canonical_encrypted_zero takes the holder key as a single\nsecp256k1_pubkey. Nothing exported parsed a single 33-byte point\n(_mpt_make_ec_pair only takes 66-byte pairs), so a JS caller had no\nclean way to build the input. --- mpt-crypto#147 ---
{"baseRefName":"main","body":"Summary\n\nResolves the three findings from the Zellic scan of mpt-crypto@3baaaac (current `main`): DEFI-1108, DEFI-1109, DEFI-1110 on the DEFI board. Each was verified against the source at that commit before anything was changed.\n\nDEFI-1110 (memory safety, the one that matters)\n\n`secp256k1_bulletproof_prove_agg` documents `*proof_len` as the caller's buffer capacity on input, but overwrites it with `proof_size` immediately after validating `m`. The only capacity check, in the serialization block, then compares that value against a locally recomputed `expected` equal to it, so `*proof_len < expected` is dead for every supported `m`, and an undersized `proof_out` is serialized past its end.\n\nReproduced under AddressSanitizer on unpatched `main`: a 600-byte heap buffer with declared capacity 600 takes a heap-buffer-overflow WRITE 0 bytes after the region, in the trailing-scalar memcpy block, for an m=1 proof requiring 688 bytes (88-byte overrun). The same repro against this branch returns 0 and reports 688.\n\nFix: capture the capacity in a local before `*proof_len` is written, and resolve the size-query (`proof_out == NULL`), NULL-`proof_len` and insufficient-capacity paths at the top of the function, before any proving work rather than after it.\n\nCompatibility: both in-repo callers (`mpt_get_bulletproof_agg`, reached from the Send and ConvertBack builders) already pass `kMPT_{SINGLE,DOUBLE}_BULLETPROOF_SIZE`, which equal the computed sizes for m=1 and m=2, so existing callers are unaffected. Two behaviour differences worth knowing: a size query now returns immediately without running the prover (previously it returned 1 only if proving succeeded), and on an internal proving failure `*proof_len` is left at the caller's capacity rather than set to the required size. Neither is relied on in-repo. Separately, note that `mpt_get_bulletproof_agg` only checks the written length after the call returns, so the utility layer was never itself enforcing the capacity it passes down; correct today because every caller hands it a matching constant.\n\nDEFI-1108 (robustness)\n\n`secp256k1_bulletproof_create_commitment` dereferenced `h_generator` and `commitment_C` without validating them, so a NULL required pointer crashed the process instead of returning the documented 0 (confirmed: SIGSEGV on unpatched `main`). Added `MPT_ARG_CHECK` entry-point checks, the idiom already used across `elgamal.c` and the proof modules. The same checks are added to `prove_agg`, which had the identical gap the scan did not flag. `context_id` is deliberately excluded: it is documented optional and NULL-guarded at every transcript absorption.\n\nDEFI-1109 (documented, deliberately not changed)\n\n`secp256k1_elgamal_add` / `_subtract` cannot represent an identity result, because a `secp256k1_pubkey` has no encoding for the point at infinity; subtracting a ciphertext from itself, or any pair sharing a component, returns 0 indistinguishably from a genuine failure. Giving the ciphertext an identity-capable representation changes the exported representation downstream consumers build on, so this PR documents the limitation in the API contract and pins it with tests instead. Whether to change the representation is a protocol decision, not a library one, and it is the same underlying mechanism as AXRT-4218 at the transactor layer, so the two are worth deciding together. Both functions are WASM-exported specifically for client-side chained-balance prediction (`CB_S' = CB_S -/+ the encrypted amount`), which is the path that can actually reach a cancellation, so the header now points callers at `generate_canonical_encrypted_zero()` for a zero balance.\n\nTesting\n\nNew `tests/test_api_hardening.c`. Verified to be genuine regression tests by building the same file against unpatched `main`: the DEFI-1108 case exits 139 (SIGSEGV) and the DEFI-1110 case exits 134 (assertion), while the DEFI-1109 case passes both before and after, since it pins existing behaviour rather than changing it.\n\nOn this branch: Debug build on macOS with `MPT_CRYPTO_WERROR=ON`, 13/13 ctest pass. `pre-commit run` clean on all changed files. The `_shared` test variant is exercised only in CI.\n","comments":[],"commits":[{"authoredDate":"2026-09-26T02:38:29Z","authors":[{"email":"redacted@example.invalid","id":"U_kgDODnE2TA","login":"tesseract-ripple","name":"the user"}],"committedDate":"2026-09-26T03:23:00Z","messageBody":"Resolves three Zellic scan findings against mpt-crypto@3baaaac.\n\nDEFI-1110 (memory safety). secp256k1_bulletproof_prove_agg documents\n*proof_len as the caller's buffer capacity on input, but overwrote it with\nproof_size immediately after validating m. The only capacity check, in the\nserialization block, then compared that value against a locally recomputed\n`expected` equal to it, so `*proof_len < expected` was dead for every\nsupported m and an undersized proof_out was serialized past its end.\n\nReproduced under ASan on the unpatched tree: a 600-byte heap buffer with a\ndeclared capacity of 600 takes a heap-buffer-overflow WRITE 0 bytes after the\nregion, in the trailing-scalar memcpy block, for an m=1 proof requiring 688\nbytes. {"body":"The README sends readers to rippled's `BUILD.md` to set up Conan. That setup runs `conan/init.sh`, which adds the `xrplf` remote at `https://conan.xrplf.org/repository/conan/` ([`init.sh#L21`](https://github.com/XRPLF/rippled/blob/8f4e9c25d892767727ca5af09599df2b47890f9a/conan/init.sh#L21)). This repository's `conan.lock` pins `openssl/3.6.2#4789bbf1…`, which that remote does not serve, so dependency resolution fails. The same command works with `https://conan.ripplex.io`, which is the remote this repository's own scripts and CI add.\n\n**Ask:** move this repository to `conan.xrplf.org`, as XRPLF/clio#3118 did for clio.\n\n**What I ran**\n\nmpt-crypto `3baaaac` (tag `1.0.5`), Conan 2.32.0, a fresh `CONAN_HOME` for each run, 2026-09-23.\n\n```sh\nconan profile detect\nbash rippled/conan/init.sh          # rippled 8f4e9c25: global.conf, profiles, xrplf remote\nconan graph info . --lockfile=conan.lock\n# exit 1\n# ERROR: Package 'openssl/3.6.2' not resolved: Unable to find\n#   'openssl/3.6.2#4789bbf131b77d0515d15e094c8f697f%1778071755.506' in remotes.\n\n# Control, with the remote this repository's scripts use:\nconan profile detect\nconan remote add --index 0 xrplf https://conan.ripplex.io\nconan graph info . --lockfile=conan.lock\n# exit 0, all three recipes \"Downloaded (xrplf)\"\n```\n\nChecked directly against each remote:\n\n```sh\ncurl https://conan.xrplf.org/repository/conan/v2/conans/openssl/3.6.2/_/_/revisions   # B. Code Quality Issues

This appendix contains findings that do not have immediate or obvious security
implications. However, addressing them may enhance the code’s readability and may
prevent the introduction of vulnerabilities in the future.

    ●​ Use of magic numbers. In several areas throughout the codebase, unnamed and
       undocumented constant values are used. This is particularly prevalent in the
       Fiat-Shamir challenge generation code, with calls such as SHA256_Update(&sha,
       last_challenge, 32). Frequently used constants like 32 or 33 should be given
       names (for instance, PUBKEY_SERIALIZED_LEN), with succinct documentation of
       their purpose and derivation. This will help reduce risks from typos and
       find-and-replace errors in the future.

    ●​ Unnecessary checks for calls to free. The
       secp256k1_bulletproof_prove_agg function includes several lines that
       conditionally call free if the relevant pointer is non-NULL. This is not necessary;
       calling free on a NULL pointer has well-defined behavior (it has no effect).

    ●​ Conditionals without brackets. Throughout the codebase, there are many
       conditional jumps to clean up code. The associated goto statements are not
       protected within blocks. This can lead to potential confusion, especially if the goto
       statements are on separate lines from the conditional statements that control their
       execution. This can lead to security failures, such as the Apple “goto fail” bug of
       2014. We recommend placing all conditionals inside brackets to prevent potential
       problems.

    ●​ Empty cleanup labels in verification functions. Several verification functions use
       cleanup: labels that do not perform any cleanup operations (such as those in
       equality_proof.c, proof_pok_sk.c, and proof_same_plaintext_multi.c).
       While this is not a security issue since verification functions process public inputs
       that do not require secure erasure, the empty cleanup labels may be misleading.

          ok = 1;

          cleanup:
          return ok;

         Figure B.1: Empty cleanup label (mpt-crypto/src/equality_proof.c#L251–L254)

        In contrast, prover functions that handle secret values properly use cleanup labels
        to securely erase sensitive data with OPENSSL_cleanse, as can be seen in
        proof_same_plaintext.c. Either the verification functions could be updated to
        remove the cleanup labels entirely and use direct returns, or the labels could be
        renamed to something like done: to clarify that no cleanup is necessary.
​
    Trail of Bits​                            53​       Ripple Labs XRP Ledger Confidential Transfer​
    PUBLIC​                                    ​                              Cryptographic Review
    ●​ Modular reduction obscured by NULL overflow parameter in scalar
       conversions. All calls to secp256k1_scalar_set_b32 in mpt_scalar.c pass
       NULL as the overflow parameter, which prevents the caller from determining
       whether the input 32-byte array was reduced modulo the group order. This
       information could be useful for debugging or validation purposes.

          void secp256k1_mpt_scalar_add(unsigned char *res, const unsigned char *a,
          const unsigned char *b) {
              secp256k1_scalar s_res, s_a, s_b;
              secp256k1_scalar_set_b32(&s_a, a, NULL);
              secp256k1_scalar_set_b32(&s_b, b, NULL);
              secp256k1_scalar_add(&s_res, &s_a, &s_b);
              secp256k1_scalar_get_b32(res, &s_res);

                             Figure B.2: NULL passed as overflow parameter
                            (mpt-crypto/src/mpt_scalar.c#L60–L65)

    ●​ Unchecked return value from scalar addition. The function
       secp256k1_mpt_scalar_add in mpt_scalar.c (excerpted in figure B.2) calls
       secp256k1_scalar_add but does not check its return value. The
       secp256k1_scalar_add function returns an integer indicating whether the
       addition overflowed the modular field (returning 1 if an overflow occurred and 0
       otherwise).

    ●​ Unchecked size parameter with unsafe cast to smaller integer type. The
       function secp256k1_mpt_get_generator_vector in commitments.c accepts a
       size_t n parameter but immediately casts it to uint32_t without validation. On
       64-bit platforms, this cast can silently truncate large values, causing the function to
       generate fewer generators than requested.

          int secp256k1_mpt_get_generator_vector(
                  const secp256k1_context* ctx,
                  secp256k1_pubkey* vec,
                  size_t n,
                  const unsigned char* label,
                  size_t label_len
          ) {
              for (uint32_t i = 0; i < (uint32_t)n; i++) {
                  if (!secp256k1_mpt_hash_to_point_nums(ctx, &vec[i], label, label_len,
          i)) {
                      return 0;
                  }
              }

                               Figure B.3: Cast of size_t to uint32_t
                          (mpt-crypto/src/commitments.c#L124–L135)

        While it is unlikely that legitimate use cases require more than four billion
        generators, the silent truncation could lead to subtle bugs.
​
    Trail of Bits​                             54​       Ripple Labs XRP Ledger Confidential Transfer​
    PUBLIC​                                     ​                              Cryptographic Review
    ●​ Missing zero check before scalar inversion. The function
       secp256k1_mpt_scalar_inverse in mpt_scalar.c does not check whether the
       input scalar is zero before invoking secp256k1_scalar_inverse. Zero has no
       multiplicative inverse in a finite field, and passing zero to the underlying function
       produces zero as the result rather than signaling an error. A caller relying on the
       output to perform a division-equivalent operation would silently receive an incorrect
       result.

          void secp256k1_mpt_scalar_inverse(unsigned char *res, const unsigned char *in)
          {
              secp256k1_scalar s;
              secp256k1_scalar_set_b32(&s, in, NULL);
              secp256k1_scalar_inverse(&s, &s);
              secp256k1_scalar_get_b32(res, &s);

                /* SECURE CLEANUP */
                OPENSSL_cleanse(&s, sizeof(s));
          }

                           Figure B.4: Inversion function allows inversion of zero
                              (mpt-crypto/src/mpt_scalar.c#L86–L94)

        The function should check whether the input is zero and return an error code
        before proceeding.

    ●​ Missing zeroization. In the function secp256k1_mpt_prove_same_plaintext in
       src/proof_same_plaintext.c, several intermediate sensitive variables—s_m,
       s_r1, s_r2, and term—are not zeroized, as shown in figure B.5.

          cleanup:
          OPENSSL_cleanse(k_m, 32);
          OPENSSL_cleanse(k_r1, 32);
          OPENSSL_cleanse(k_r2, 32);
          OPENSSL_cleanse(m_scalar, 32);
          return ok;

                              Figure B.5: Only a few variables are zeroized.
                      (mpt-crypto/src/proof_same_plaintext.c#L193–L198)

    ●​ Undocumented optional context ID in challenge computation. The internal
       challenge hash computation functions (such as build_link_challenge_hash in
       proof_link.c and build_same_plaintext_hash_input in
       proof_same_plaintext.c) silently accept a NULL context_id, omitting it from
       the hash when absent.

          if (context_id) {
              SHA256_Update(&sha, context_id, 32);
          }

​
    Trail of Bits​                               55​        Ripple Labs XRP Ledger Confidential Transfer​
    PUBLIC​                                       ​                               Cryptographic Review
            Figure B.6: Optional context_id (mpt-crypto/src/proof_link.c#L98–L100)

        This optionality is not reflected in the public API signatures, which declare
        context_id as a plain const unsigned char* with no indication that NULL is a
        valid input or that it changes the challenge computation. This field is also crucial to
        the security of the higher-level confidential transaction protocol and should
        probably not be considered optional.

    ●​ Discrepancy between implementation and specification for zero-handling. The
       underlying function secp256k1_ec_seckey_verify rejects zero, but the
       specification permits it as randomness. For example, under the prover steps of
       section 3.3.4, “Proof of Equality of Plaintexts with Shared Randomness,” the random
       blinding scalar are sampled as the following:

                                             𝑘𝑚, 𝑘𝑟 ← $ ℤ𝑞

        The above technically allows zero to be sampled, while the implementation rejects it.
        This small discrepancy does not have serious practical implications.

    ●​ Misleading constant name ecGamalEncryptedLength. The function
       ecGamalEncryptedLength defined in Protocol.h is defined as 33 bytes and
       documented as “EC ElGamal ciphertext length.” In reality, this is the length of a
       compressed secp256k1 point. An EC–ElGamal ciphertext is composed of a pair of
       such elliptic curve points. The constant ecGamalEncryptedLength, therefore,
       names a compressed curve point, not a ciphertext, and is used throughout
       ConfidentialTransfer.cpp precisely in that role. A name such as
       ecCompressedPointLength would more accurately reflect what the constant
       measures and prevent future readers from conflating a single group element with a
       full ElGamal ciphertext.

    ●​ Missing zeroization of blinding factor in generateBlindingFactor. The
       function generateBlindingFactor allocates a stack buffer, fills it with a
       cryptographically random scalar via RAND_bytes, copies it into a heap-allocated
       Buffer, and returns. The stack buffer is never explicitly zeroed out before the
       function returns. The blinding factor is the ElGamal encryption randomness r;
       disclosure of r allows anyone who knows it to decrypt any ciphertext produced with
       that randomness by computing C2 − r·pk = mG and solving the discrete logarithm
       for small m.




​
    Trail of Bits​                             56​        Ripple Labs XRP Ledger Confidential Transfer​
    PUBLIC​                                     ​                               Cryptographic Review
C. Performance Improvement Opportunities

--- Appendix C ---
This appendix discusses areas where we noted opportunities to improve efficiency and
performance. These observations do not relate to security or maintainability, only to
opportunities to make proving and verification run more quickly.

scalar_pow_u32 Issues
                                            𝑦
The scalar_pow_u32 function computes 𝑥 mod 𝑝, where 𝑝 is the secp256k1 curve order.
It uses iterated multiplication, multiplying 𝑥 by itself 𝑦 times in a loop. Assuming a
variable-time implementation is acceptable, a simple square-and-multiply algorithm uses
about 48 operations per exponentiation on average.

The secp256k1_verify_bulletproof_agg function calls scalar_pow_u32 inside
several loops. These loops compute successive powers of the same value; that is, they
              𝑖   𝑖+1   𝑖+2   𝑖+𝑘
compute 𝑥 , 𝑥 , 𝑥 , ..., 𝑥 . Over 64 iterations, this will result in about 2,000 scalar
multiplications. If the power were computed iteratively in the outer loops, this would be
reduced to somewhere between 64 and 70 multiplications (depending on the starting
power).

We recommend updating the loops to use successive multiplications inside each loop. If
scalar_pow_u32 is expected to be used elsewhere with values larger than 48, consider
switching over to a square-and-multiply algorithm.

Recomputation of H Generator
The function secp256k1_mpt_get_h_generator recomputes a constant on every call.
The function derives the H generator by running a try-and-increment hash loop (in
commitments.c), hashing the fixed inputs "MPT_BULLETPROOF_V1_NUMS", "secp256k1",
"H", index 0, and a counter until a valid curve point is found. Because all inputs are
constants, the resulting point is always the same. Therefore, the SHA-256 computation and
curve point parsing are performed redundantly on every call, including every time a
commitment or proof function is invoked. The resulting point could instead be computed
once offline, hard-coded as a 33-byte compressed constant, and parsed at startup or lazily
cached, eliminating the repeated work entirely.




​
    Trail of Bits​                              57​    Ripple Labs XRP Ledger Confidential Transfer​
    PUBLIC​                                      ​                           Cryptographic Review
D. API Improvement Opportunities

The mpt-crypto library implements cryptographically sound primitives for confidential
transactions on the XRP Ledger. While the core cryptographic operations are correct, the
API design has several areas that could be improved to enhance safety, usability, and
maintainability. This document provides actionable recommendations for hardening the
library interface before deployment.

Missing or Inconsistent Parameter Validation
Most API functions do not validate input pointers before dereferencing them. If a caller
passes NULL, the library will segfault rather than returning an error. Consider the
secp256k1_elgamal_decrypt function, shown in figure D.1, which does not check the
validity of any of its pointer parameters before using them.

    int secp256k1_elgamal_decrypt(
            const secp256k1_context* ctx,
            uint64_t* amount,
            const secp256k1_pubkey* c1,
            const secp256k1_pubkey* c2,
            const unsigned char* privkey
    ) {
        secp256k1_pubkey S, M_target, current_M, G_point, next_M;
        const secp256k1_pubkey* pts[2];
        uint64_t i;
        unsigned char one[32] = {0}; one[31] = 1;

        /* 1. Recover Shared Secret: S = privkey * C1 */
        S = *c1;
        if (!secp256k1_ec_pubkey_tweak_mul(ctx, &S, privkey)) return 0;

                       Figure D.1: Missing NULL pointer checks in ElGamal encryption
                               (mpt-crypto/src/elgamal.c#L111–L125)

In addition, semantic validation of parameters is performed inconsistently across the
codebase. For example, the prover function
secp256k1_mpt_prove_same_plaintext_multi does not validate the input
randomness array r_array before using it, while the function
secp256k1_elgamal_pedersen_link_prove validates its witness parameter:

--- Appendix D ---

    /* 0. Validate Witnesses */
    if (!secp256k1_ec_seckey_verify(ctx, r)) return 0;

                Figure D.2: Witness validation in link proof that is missing in other functions
                             (mpt-crypto/src/proof_link.c#L131–L132)




​
      Trail of Bits​                                  58​        Ripple Labs XRP Ledger Confidential Transfer​
      PUBLIC​                                          ​                               Cryptographic Review
Recommendation: Add comprehensive NULL pointer checks and parameter validation at
the start of all public API functions to prevent undefined behavior and enable graceful error
handling.

Implicit Length Assumptions
Many functions accept byte array pointers with implicit length assumptions documented
only in comments; an example is shown in figure D.3. This creates a risk of buffer
over-reads if callers provide incorrectly sized buffers.

    int generate_canonical_encrypted_zero(
            const secp256k1_context* ctx,
            secp256k1_pubkey* enc_zero_c1,
            secp256k1_pubkey* enc_zero_c2,
            const secp256k1_pubkey* pubkey,
            const unsigned char* account_id,     // 20 bytes
            const unsigned char* mpt_issuance_id // 24 bytes

                       Figure D.3: Fixed-size byte arrays without explicit length parameters
                                  (mpt-crypto/src/elgamal.c#L211–L217)

Proving and verifying functions similarly accept proof buffers with lengths that are only
documented in the prover implementation rather than validated at the API boundary:

    /**
     * Verifies the proof of equality with shared randomness.
     */
    int secp256k1_mpt_verify_equality_shared_r(
            const secp256k1_context* ctx,
            const unsigned char* proof,
            size_t n,
            const secp256k1_pubkey* C1,
            const secp256k1_pubkey* C2_vec,
            const secp256k1_pubkey* Pk_vec,
            const unsigned char* context_id

                          Figure D.4: Variable-length arrays without bounds validation
                          (mpt-crypto/include/secp256k1_mpt.h#L436–L446)

Recommendation: Add explicit length parameters for all buffer arguments and validate
them at function entry to prevent buffer overflows and over-reads.

Generic Error Codes
Currently, functions return only 0 for error and 1 for success, with no distinction between
different failure modes. This makes debugging difficult, as callers cannot distinguish
between NULL pointer errors, invalid inputs, cryptographic failures, or out-of-range values.

Recommendation: Consider introducing structured error codes (e.g., -1 for NULL pointer,
-2 for invalid input, -3 for crypto failure), as shown in figure D.5.
​
      Trail of Bits​                                    59​        Ripple Labs XRP Ledger Confidential Transfer​
      PUBLIC​                                            ​                               Cryptographic Review
    #define SECP256K1_MPT_OK                    1
    #define SECP256K1_MPT_ERROR                 0
    #define SECP256K1_MPT_ERROR_NULL_POINTER   -1
    #define SECP256K1_MPT_ERROR_INVALID_INPUT -2
    #define SECP256K1_MPT_ERROR_CRYPTO_FAILED -3
    #define SECP256K1_MPT_ERROR_OUT_OF_RANGE   -4
    #define SECP256K1_MPT_ERROR_BUFFER_TOO_SMALL -5

                             Figure D.5: Suggested return code convention

Incomplete Parameter Documentation
The API header file lacks comprehensive documentation for function parameters. Many
functions do not specify whether parameters are input or output, what the required buffer
sizes are, whether pointers can be NULL, or what specific values cause failures.

    int secp256k1_bulletproof_verify_agg(
            const secp256k1_context* ctx,
            const secp256k1_pubkey* G_vec,                   /* length n = 64*m */
            const secp256k1_pubkey* H_vec,                   /* length n = 64*m */
            const unsigned char* proof,
            size_t proof_len,
            const secp256k1_pubkey* commitment_C_vec,        /* length m */
            size_t m,
            const secp256k1_pubkey* pk_base,
            const unsigned char* context_id

                              Figure D.6: Missing function documentation
                       (mpt-crypto/include/secp256k1_mpt.h#L459–L468)

Recommendation: Adopt comprehensive Doxygen-style documentation specifying
input/output semantics, required buffer sizes, NULL-ability, thread safety, side-channel
properties, and specific failure conditions for all public API functions.

Adoption of a Unified Coding Standard
The codebase lacks consistent coding conventions for control flow and error handling.
Some functions use multiple return statements throughout the code, while others use
single-exit patterns with goto cleanup. Some functions validate inputs incrementally,
while others batch validation at the start. An example is shown in figure D.7.

    free(L_vec);
    free(R_vec);

    return ok ? 1 : 0;

    fail:
    free(L_vec);
    free(R_vec);
    return 0;


​
      Trail of Bits​                             60​        Ripple Labs XRP Ledger Confidential Transfer​
      PUBLIC​                                     ​                               Cryptographic Review
                     Figure D.7: Inconsistent error handling and control flow patterns
                   (mpt-crypto/src/bulletproof_aggregated.c#L2178–L2186)

Recommendation: Establish and document unified coding standards covering parameter
validation order, error handling patterns (single-exit versus multiple returns), memory
allocation strategy, and naming conventions to improve code consistency and
maintainability.

Lack of Unified Data Structures
Proofs are stored as separate arrays of values that are passed individually to proof
generation and verification functions (see figure D.8, for example). This fragmented
approach reduces API ergonomics and creates opportunities for integration errors when
callers must manually coordinate multiple related parameters.

    SECP256K1_API int secp256k1_mpt_prove_same_plaintext_multi(
            const secp256k1_context* ctx,
            unsigned char* proof_out,
            size_t* proof_len,
            uint64_t amount_m,
            size_t n_ciphertexts,
            const secp256k1_pubkey* R_array,
            const secp256k1_pubkey* S_array,
            const secp256k1_pubkey* Pk_array,
            const unsigned char* r_array, // Flat array: r1 || r2 || ... (N * 32 bytes)
            const unsigned char* tx_context_id
    );

                        Figure D.8: Related proof values passed as separate arrays
                       (mpt-crypto/include/secp256k1_mpt.h#L239–L250)

In the example, the caller must manually ensure that R_array, S_array, Pk_array, and
r_array all have exactly n_ciphertexts elements. If these arrays have mismatched
lengths, the function will read out of bounds or use incorrect data, but the API provides no
mechanism to detect this error at compile time or runtime.

Similarly, proof outputs are returned as raw byte buffers rather than structured types,
requiring callers to understand the internal binary layout.

Recommendation: Introduce structured types that collect related values (e.g.,
secp256k1_mpt_ciphertext grouping R and S components, secp256k1_mpt_proof
wrapping proof buffers) to improve readability, enable compile-time validation, and provide
a more ergonomic API to library users.

Non-Distinguished Data Types
Throughout the mpt-crypto codebase, the secp256k1_pubkey type is consistently used
to represent elliptic curve points used more generally in ElGamal encryption, Pedersen

​
      Trail of Bits​                               61​       Ripple Labs XRP Ledger Confidential Transfer​
      PUBLIC​                                       ​                              Cryptographic Review
commitments, Bulletproofs, and other contexts. However, it is also used in other portions
of the protocol codebase to represent actual public keys. This can lead to confusion and
variable misuse, potentially putting sensitive values at risk.

Recommendation: Create different types to separate distinct uses of the same data
structures. From a performance and compile-time perspective, typedef is free. However,
when coupled with suitable compiler flags (such as -Wpedantic), it can identify when
cross-uses of the same data structure occur, catching potential errors. Since valid
cross-uses can be resolved by casting, false positives can be easily resolved.

words=re.findall(r\"\\b[\\w][\\w'’.-]*\\b\",body)
print(len(words))

     5	
     6	`build-wasm.sh` compiles `src/utility/mpt_utility.cpp` as C++, then links the module with plain `emcc`. From emsdk 6.0.6, `emcc` no longer links the C++ runtime by default, so the link fails with the C++ runtime missing. CI did not catch it because it pins emsdk 6.0.2, and the WASM job [passed at `3baaaac`](https://github.com/XRPLF/mpt-crypto/actions/runs/32380884181). The script's own prerequisites line names no version, so a build with any emsdk I tried from 6.0.6 on fails.
     7	
     8	**Suggested fix:** link with `em++` on the three link lines, as Emscripten's changelog recommends.
     9	
    10	**Cause**
    11	
    12	Emscripten's [ChangeLog for 6.0.6](https://github.com/emscripten-core/emscripten/blob/6.0.6/ChangeLog.md#L23-L25):
    13	
    14	> `DEFAULT_TO_CXX` is now disabled by default. This means that `em++` is now required when linking C++ programs, matching the behavior of clang and gcc. The old behavior is still available using `-sDEFAULT_TO_CXX`. (#11121)
    15	
    16	At `3baaaac`:
    17	
    18	- The C++ object is compiled with `em++` ([line 272](https://github.com/XRPLF/mpt-crypto/blob/3baaaac33dadec3e649846e487f452c5b6b2d825/.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh#L272)).
    19	- It is linked with `emcc` ([lines 363, 365 and 374](https://github.com/XRPLF/mpt-crypto/blob/3baaaac33dadec3e649846e487f452c5b6b2d825/.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh#L363-L374)).
    20	- CI pins [emsdk 6.0.2](https://github.com/XRPLF/mpt-crypto/blob/3baaaac33dadec3e649846e487f452c5b6b2d825/.github/workflows/build-wasm.yml#L38-L43).
    21	- The script asks only for [*"Emscripten SDK … on PATH"*](https://github.com/XRPLF/mpt-crypto/blob/3baaaac33dadec3e649846e487f452c5b6b2d825/.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh#L19).
    22	
    23	**What I ran**
    24	
    25	`./.github/scripts/build-wasm.sh` at `3baaaac`, then `test-wasm.sh` where the table says so. Each build used a fresh source tree and its own emsdk. macOS arm64, 2026-10-18. I did not run Linux.
    26	
    27	```text
    28	emsdk                as shipped          em++ on the 3 link lines     -sDEFAULT_TO_CXX on the 3 link lines
    29	6.0.2                builds, tests pass  builds, tests pass,          builds, tests pass,
    30	                                         output byte-identical        output byte-identical
    31	6.0.3, 6.0.4, 6.0.5  builds, tests pass  not run                      not run
    32	6.0.6                fails at line 363   builds, tests pass           builds, tests pass
    33	6.0.10               fails at line 363   builds, tests pass           builds, tests pass
    34	6.0.12 (newest)      fails at line 363   builds, tests pass           builds, tests pass
    35	```
    36	
    37	The 6.0.6 and 6.0.10 failure. At 6.0.12 it also lists `__cxa_guard_acquire` and `__cxa_guard_release`.
    38	
    39	```text
    40	wasm-ld: error: …/mpt_crypto.wasm.lto.o: undefined symbol: operator new(unsigned long)
    41	  (and 7 more: operator delete, __cxa_allocate_exception, __cxa_throw,
    42	   typeinfo / vtable / ~length_error for std::length_error, std::logic_error::logic_error)
    43	emcc: warning: link failed with undefined C++ symbols. Try linking with 'em++' or passing '-sDEFAULT_TO_CXX'
    44	```
    45	
    46	All three lines need the change. With only lines 363 and 365 changed, 6.0.6 still fails at line 374.
    47

    31	---
    32	
    33	## 0. First, the two cheap rejections — before any source is opened
    34	
    35	⚠ **These cost one API call each and can end the review immediately.** Verifying
    36	claims against primary sources and the Trail of Bits appendix is the expensive
    37	part; do not spend it on a draft that cannot be filed anyway.
    38	
    39	**Right venue?** Confirm the destination repository exists and is owned by the
    40	org the draft assumes. ⚠ **Testing this on U-54 found exactly that defect** — the
    41	wallet standard is not in `XRPLF/XRPL-Standards` at all. **A correct finding sent
    42	to the wrong repository is still a wasted filing**, and no evidence check catches
    43	it.
    44	
    45	**Right shape?** Read the target's `.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/` **and decode its
    46	`config.yml`** — listing filenames does not establish a policy.
    47	
    48	```sh
    49	gh api repos/<owner>/<repo>/contents/.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/config.yml \
    50	  --jq '.content' | base64 -d
    51	```
    52	
    53	⚠ **`blank_issues_enabled: false` means only the listed shapes are accepted.**
    54	`rippled` sets it, `rippled#8118` fit neither template, and it was closed without
    55	comment. **If the draft fits no accepted template, stop here and say so.**
    56	
    57	---
    58	
    59	## ⚠ Then correctness, and this is the whole point
    60	
    61	A concision pass would not have caught **a single one** of the thirteen defects
    62	found in the 2026-08-26 round. Every one was a claim that was **false or
    63	overstated**, and counting words finds none of those.
    64	
    65	**So attack the claims before you touch the prose.**
    66	
    67	### Verify every claim against the primary source, now
    68	
    69	- Open the source the draft cites. **Do not trust the draft's quotation** — a
    70	  filed row once quoted a sentence the specification has never contained
    71	  (`UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md` U-15).
    72	- Check line numbers and section numbers still resolve. Sources move.
    73	- ⚠ **Every negative claim needs its search, and you run it.** *"There is no
    74	  such field"*, *"not called out anywhere"*, *"not exported"* — one search
    75	  settles it. **`rippled#8118` said a property was not advertised anywhere; the
    90	
    91	⚠ A draft that presents the second or third as the first is the defect that
    92	needed four public corrections. Say which layer each claim is on.
    93	
    94	⚠ **Do not let the draft's framing set yours.** Ask what would have to be true
    95	for the claim to be *wrong*, and go and check that.
    96	
    97	## Then the maintainer's read
    98	
    99	**Actionable?** Can a maintainer tell what to investigate, decide or change?
   100	
   101	**Scoped?** One independently actionable problem, or several disguised as one?
   102	
   103	**Skimmable?** Headings, bullets, code blocks, tables. Conclusion before
   104	evidence.
   105	
   106	**Something to do?** ⚠ A positives-only filing does not belong on a bug tracker
   107	at all — reject it rather than editing it.
   108	
   109	## Delete
   110	
   111	Essay introductions, throat-clearing, praise before the report, rhetorical
   112	questions, speculative motivation about maintainer intent, repeated conclusions,
   113	research narration, background a maintainer already has, and any restatement of
   114	the same point in summary and conclusion.
   115	
   116	**Also delete, specifically for this project:** project shorthand
   117	(`tapFailHard`), internal register references (`CLAIMS.md`, `E-nnn`,
   118	`REVIEW-nnn`), and this repository's ⚠-and-bold emphasis style.
   119	
   120	## Rewrite
   121	
   122	Produce the edited version. **Aim to cut at least 20% without losing
   123	load-bearing evidence.** If the draft is already tight, say so and do not cut
   124	mechanically.
   125	
   126	Run `make check-filing FILE=<draft>` on the result.
   127	
   128	## Report
   129	
   130	1. **Claims that did not survive verification** — with what you ran.
   131	2. **Claims that need a search that has not been run.**
   132	3. **Layer corrections** — demonstrated / inferred / proposed.
   133	4. **The edited version.**
   134	5. **Word count before and after.**
   135	
   136	⚠ **If any claim failed verification, say plainly that the draft is not ready to
   137	file.** Rule 1 gates on this. It is better to lose a filing than to correct one
   138	in public.
    55	produce a correction, and a correction is a public record that the filing was
    56	not ready.
    57	
    58	**The reviewer is given the draft and the sources, not the conclusion.**
    59	
    60	### 2. A filing is at most 300 words, with one concrete ask
    61	
    62	Counted over the issue body, excluding code blocks, quoted source and the
    63	disclosure footer.
    64	
    65	⚠ **`WORD_LIMIT` in `tools/check-filing.ts` is what enforces this, and the gate
    66	prints the number it used.** This heading states the rule; that constant is the
    67	instrument. If the two ever disagree, the gate is right and this line is the
    68	defect — and neither the skills nor a draft may restate the number at all.
    69	
    70	- **One ask per issue.** If there are five asks, that is five issues or one

Full review comments:

- [P2] Limit the audit claim to the searched repository — $USER_TMP/T/f12r1.uPcsX6/draft.md:64-69

Therefore “the only match” is false as written. Scope this to `XRPLF/mpt-crypto` and WebAssembly-relevant results, or add a repository-scoped search; this follows `REVIEW-INSTRUCTIONS.md:73-75`.

  The title and opening wording can read as a cross-platform claim, but every reproduction was macOS arm64 and only 6.0.6, 6.0.10, and 6.0.12 were independently exercised. The changelog establishes the tool behavior, not failures on every host; rewrite this as the tested macOS arm64 result and avoid implying Linux was tested.
